Reason in a direct trace-first way.

A productive pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Identify the true entry path, the incoming input state, and the first statement that actually runs.
- Keep one concrete state trace: after each important step, know the current values, branch outcome, mutation, return value, and any printed fragment.
- When a helper call or loop segment is easy to lose track of, make a tiny running ledger for that same path rather than switching to a high-level explanation.
- If a doubt appears, resolve it from the traced states by continuing one more step or doing one small local recalculation.
- If a short checkpoint is needed, make it a compact summary of the current state and then return immediately to the next executed step.
- Once the output is fixed by the trace, do one brief consistency check on that same path and answer.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- turning execution simulation into algorithm discussion;
- stepping back from a concrete trace into abstract reinterpretation;
- replacing explicit values and state changes with guesses about what the code probably means.

```
import hashlib
import inspect
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Callable, Optional

from nicegui import background_tasks, functions

def hash_file_path(path: Path) -> str:
    return hashlib.md5(str(path).encode()).hexdigest()[:8]

def compute_key(path: Path) -> str:
    nicegui_base = Path(__file__).parent
    is_file = path.is_file()
    try:
        path = path.relative_to(nicegui_base)
    except ValueError:
        pass
    if is_file:
        return f'{hash_file_path(path.parent)}/{path.name}'
    return f'{hash_file_path(path)}'
```

MD5 algorithm:
- Append padding: message bit length ≡ 448 mod 512. Append 0x80, then zeros, then 64-bit little-endian length in bits.
- Process 512-bit blocks.
- Initial state: A=0x67452301, B=0xefcdab89, C=0x98badcfe, D=0x10325476.
- For each block, copy state to a,b,c,d. Perform 64 operations:
  - F = (b & c) | (~b & d) for rounds 1; G = (d & b) | (~d & c) for round 2; H = b ^ c ^ d; I = c ^ (b | ~d).
  - a = b + ((a + F + K[i] + M[g]) <<< s[i])
  - etc.

The code snippet:
```
def compute_key(path: Path) -> str:
    
    nicegui_base = Path(__file__).parent
    is_file = path.is_file()
    try:
        path = path.relative_to(nicegui_base)
    except ValueError:
        pass
    if is_file:
        return f'{hash_file_path(path.parent)}/{path.name}'
    return f'{hash_file_path(path)}'
```

Shifts:
Round1: s[0..15] = 7,12,17,22,7,12,17,22,7,12,17,22,7,12,17,22
Round2: s[16..31] = 5,9,14,20,5,9,14,20,5,9,14,20,5,9,14,20
Round3: s[32..47] = 4,11,16,23,4,11,16,23,4,11,16,23,4,11,16,23

Message index order:
Round1: g = i (0..15)
Round2: g = (5*i + 1) mod 16
Round3: g = (3*i + 5) mod 16
Round4: g = (7*i) mod 16

We need process Block 1 then Block 2. Initial state:
A0=0x67452301
B0=0xefcdab89
C0=0x98badcfe
D0=0x10325476

We'll maintain a,b,c,d. After each operation:
temp = d; d = c; c = b; b = b + leftrotate((a + F + K[i] + M[g]), s[i]); a = temp.
Actually MD5 operation:
a = b + ((a + F(b,c,d) + K[i] + M[g]) <<< s)
Then rotate assignments: (a,b,c,d) = (d, a, b, c)? Wait standard MD5:
For each round:
A = B + ((A + F(B,C,D) + K[i] + M[g]) <<< s)
Then (A,B,C,D) = (D, A, B, C) after each operation? Let's recall.

MD5 compression:
```
for i in range(64):
    if i < 16: F = (B & C) | (~B & D); g = i
    elif i < 32: F = (D & B) | (~D & C); g = (5*i + 1) % 16
    elif i < 48: F = B ^ C ^ D; g = (3*i + 5) % 16
    else: F = C ^ (B | ~D); g = (7*i) % 16
    F = F + A + K[i] + M[g]
    A = D
    D = C
    C = B
    B = B + leftrotate(F, s[i])
```
Actually the assignment is:
```
A, D, C, B = D, C, B, B + leftrotate(F, s[i])
```
Let's verify. In RFC 1321:
```
FF(a,b,c,d,Mj,s,Ki) { a = b + ((a + F(b,c,d) + Mj + Ki) <<< s); }
```
Then in the round:
```
FF(a,b,c,d,M0,7,0xd76aa478)
FF(d,a,b,c,M1,12,0xe8c7b756)
FF(c,d,a,b,M2,17,0x242070db)
FF(b,c,d,a,M3,22,0xc1bdceee)
...
```
So the variable order rotates: after each operation, the roles shift: a becomes d? Actually the macro updates `a` in place, and the next call uses `d,a,b,c` as (a,b,c,d). So after operation, the new tuple is (d, a_old? Wait if call FF(a,b,c,d) updates a, then next call FF(d,a,b,c) uses the updated a as the second argument? Let's derive.

Standard MD5 round:
```
for i in range(0,16):
    a = b + ((a + F(b,c,d) + K[i] + M[g]) <<< s[i])
    d = a? No.
```
Actually the code in many implementations:
```
for i in range(64):
    if i < 16:
        F = (b & c) | (~b & d)
        g = i
    ...
    F = F + a + K[i] + M[g]
    a, d, c, b = d, c, b, b + leftrotate(F, s[i])
```
Let's check with known initial and first operation. For MD5("") = d41d8cd98f00b204e9800998ecf8427e. Let's test memory: First operation:
a=0x67452301, b=0xefcdab89, c=0x98badcfe, d=0x10325476.
F = (b & c) | (~b & d). Then a_new = b + ((a + F + K0 + M0) <<< 7). Then new state should be a=d, b=a_new, c=b, d=c? Wait standard after first op, the variables for next op are (d, a_new, b, c)? Let's recall from RFC:
```
FF(a,b,c,d,M0,7,0xd76aa478)  // a = b + ((a + F(b,c,d) + M0 + K0) <<< 7)
FF(d,a,b,c,M1,12,0xe8c7b756) // d = a + ((d + F(a,b,c) + M1 + K1) <<< 12)
FF(c,d,a,b,M2,17,0x242070db) // c = d + ((c + F(d,a,b) + M2 + K2) <<< 17)
FF(b,c,d,a,M3,22,0xc1bdceee) // b = c + ((b + F(c,d,a) + M3 + K3) <<< 22)
...
```
So after first FF, `a` is updated. Then second FF uses `d` as the "a" argument and `a` (updated) as the "b" argument. So the state tuple after first operation is (d, a_new, b, c) in terms of (a,b,c,d) for next? Actually the arguments for second call are (d, a, b, c) where `a` is the updated a from first call, `b` is original b, `c` is original c. So the new state is a=d, b=a_new, c=b_old, d=c_old. So yes, assignment: a=d; b=a_new; c=b_old; d=c_old. Equivalent to `a, d, c, b = d, c, b, b + leftrotate(...)`? Let's see: If we do `a, d, c, b = d, c, b, b + rot`, then new a=old d, new d=old c, new c=old b, new b=old b + rot. That matches (a=d, b=new, c=old b, d=old c). Yes.

So the operation formula:
```
F = F(b,c,d)
temp = b + leftrotate((a + F + K[i] + M[g]), s[i])
a, d, c, b = d, c, b, temp
```
Or equivalently:
```
old_a=a, old_b=b, old_c=c, old_d=d
a = old_d
d = old_c
c = old_b
b = old_b + leftrotate((old_a + F(old_b,old_c,old_d) + K[i] + M[g]), s[i])
```
We'll use this.

Need be careful with 32-bit overflow. We'll do modular addition mod 2^32. Rotate left.
